by thelionandthemoon

Summary

“She just walked out the store with a TV and insisted we call you.” The head security guard told Rio.

He opened a laptop and played her the footage.

“Right.” She rubbed her forehead. “Why would you do that?” She asked.

“I’ve been a bad girl, officer. You must arrest me.” Agatha replied in a breathy voice.

Of course, she would make a show of shoplifting a television just to flirt with her.

AU: Police Officer Rio and Petty Criminal Agatha

Notes

this came to me in a dream (agatha should be allowed to be a chaotic bitch while rio sort of tries to follow rules) hope you enjoy it

Chapter 1

Rio moved from New York City to Westview for one very simple reason: she wanted to work less – no, not exactly less, she was working the exact same number of hours as all police officers were required to in the state of New Jersey – she merely wanted work to be easy, and what is easier than a small residential town where nothing happens?

She had not once regretted her decision, much too happy to leave the chaos of the city behind. She neither missed the traffic nor the people, she thought, driving down Main Street in the middle of the day.

Rio waved back at an elderly couple crossing the street and smiled at a kid holding a nice labradoodle on a leash under the careful eye of his mother. She loved Westview. It was quiet.

She parked the police car and walked into the coffee shop. She could waste five minutes of her patrol on a Tuesday afternoon: nothing would happen.

“Looking good, officer.” She heard. When Rio turned to the voice, she was met with the one and only Agatha Harkness. The bane of her existence. The harbinger of chaos.

“Hello, Agatha.” She replied simply.

The older woman winked and walked away. She was wearing business-casual clothes: a shirt and high-waisted pants that fit her so well Rio could hardly tear her eyes away from them. She was probably on her way to scam upstanding Westview citizens, the officer guessed. She really had no idea what Harkness did for work, probably something illegal, if she even had a job.

That woman was trouble. A very attractive sort of trouble who would relentlessly flirt with her. It took everything Rio had not to cave and wipe that smug smirk off her face with her own lips.

Rio sighed and shook herself awake from her Agatha-induced daydream; a coffee would do her good.

She cursed the woman under her breath – Rio refused to jeopardise her position in this quaint city by sleeping with a known-player and criminal.

The barista who made her coffee smiled at her politely and wished her a good shift. She would never give this up, not for a few hours of pleasure between Agatha Harkness’s thighs.

Not ever.

She spotted the she-devil sitting on a bench with a lit cigarette in her hand not far from where Rio had parked. She looked deviously proud which the officer found suspicious, but she could hardly arrest her for smiling too smugly.

She chose to ignore her and simply made her way to her car. Her spotless, beautiful police car that had been vandalised in the five minutes she had spent away from it.

Rio stared at the pair of breasts drawn with what she assumed to be a black marker.

With the napkin the waitress had handed her with the coffee, she tried to wipe it off. It worked instantly. The ink was merely whiteboard marker.

“Seriously?” She said.

Behind her, Agatha started cackling. “Why did you do this, Agatha?” She asked, turning to her menacingly.

“Moi?” Agatha brought a hand to her chest and gasped in feigned shock. “I would never do anything like that.”

“You’re saying I won’t find a whiteboard marker in your pockets if I look?”

“You can frisk me whenever, officer. You don’t need to accuse me of anything for that.” She smiled.

Rio just exhaled. “Get a job, Harkness.” She said before getting in her car. She could still hear Agatha’s taunting cackling even with the door closed.

That woman was infuriating, always causing trouble, but never quite enough to be charged or convicted of anything.

On Rio’s first week in Westview, Agatha had started a bar fight: a man had, allegedly, touched her hip and grinded too close so she broke his wrist and punched his two friends who had come to his defence. She was arrested and kept in custody for the entire night after threatening the men with more bodily harm. The three idiots did not press charges and Agatha walked free after having spent hours flirting with Rio.

A month later, Agatha had set fire to a family of four’s bed of tulips in the middle of the night. She had followed Officer Vidal to the station in handcuffs, making raunchy comments that bordered on sexual harassment. Rio had filed no complaint. How hypocritical of her to do so when she had silently pictured the very same things all night.

The family had not pressed charges, in fact, the husband claimed he had accidently set fire to his own flowers even though Agatha was caught red-handed by firefighters and Rio herself.

A fortnight later, Agatha was arrested for egging the mayor’s home. She was let go on the orders of the mayor herself. Rio had no idea what that had all been about, all she knew was that Harkness had spent the whole time winking and blowing kisses in her direction.

It had happened a few more times: petty crime (harmless vandalism of her neighbours’ cars), disorderly conduct after a night out (bar fights), theft of public property (road signs) – and every time, Agatha had walked out of the station free of any charge.

Rio’s colleagues merely shrugged at her antics, offering to drive her home instead of arresting her. What was the point, really? She did not always accept, preferring to threaten them with more disorderly behaviour if she were to be released early.

Agatha Harkness was as obnoxiously unbearable as she was attractive. And to Rio, that made her dangerous. She had always loved the crazy ones, the morally reprehensible, the psychos. If “her type” could be personified, it would be Agatha.

But instead of succumbing to the she-devil’s charms, Rio pretended to be irritated by them. (She really hoped her colleagues did not suspect anything; she would hate to lose this perfectly boring job.)

Officer Vidal’s shift ended early enough for her to stop by the library after picking her card up from the townhall. Why the library itself did not deliver them was a mystery to her. Apparently, the person who ran it was perfectly rude and deterred anyone who tried to sign up for one. Rio could not imagine who would be off-putting enough for that.

She entered the building and was surprised to find it empty: no one to greet her at the counter. She imagined the librarian had to be busy somewhere. It didn’t matter.

Rio was also surprised by the collection: an entire section on witchcraft. She picked an old spell book to go with the botanical anthology she wished to borrow. The LGBTQ+ shelves appeared well-stocked too, she promised to browse them next time.

And finally, after one last look at the well-arranged books and studious teenagers at the computers, she walked to the counter.

That’s where she saw her, her worst nightmare, Agatha Harkness, sitting on a highchair behind the computer. She had a nametag that seemed purposefully indecipherable.

“What are you doing here?” Rio asked, suspicious.

“I work here.” Agatha shrugged.

“You’re a librarian?” Rio asked again.

She looked bewildered which riled Agatha up.

“No, I’m a drug dealer and this is a great spot.” Harkness replied sarcastically.

“Is this community service or something?” Rio questioned again. It just did not add up: Agatha Harkness, the troublemaker, running the library.

“Jesus, are you actually here for a book or just to waste my time?” The other woman bit back.

Rio almost stepped away, looking at Agatha with her wide doe eyes. She was intimidating, it made her heart start and her insides clench on nothing. She willed herself to ignore this reaction. Now was not a good time.

“I’d like to borrow these.” She said, handing her the two books.

“Plants and witchcraft. That’s hot.” Agatha commented.

“Okay.” Rio sighed. There she was, good old Agatha.

“Do you want to join my coven? Everyone keeps saying no. Apparently dancing naked around a bonfire in the forest is not appealing to the community.” The woman offered with an innocent smile. She handed Rio her books and card, batting her eyelashes comically.

“Have a good day, Agatha.” The officer replied, ignoring her once again. She hurried out of the library.

“Come again soon, officer.” Harkness called from the counter. “Love, Simon, really? You’ve read it four times. We have plenty of other gay books.” Rio heard her say.

“It’s my favourite.” A teenager replied sheepishly. It was one of the Maximoff boys, the one who wore eyeliner and drank too much iced coffee.

It took everything in her not to tell him stop being so stereotypically gay. Thankfully, she knew to keep her comments to herself. As well as other things, she sighed, closing the door behind her.

Note to herself: never go to the library, instead destroy the small bookshop community and your own savings account by buying off Amazon.

She drove home, a one-bedroom house across town with a yard consequential enough for her to plant all the flowers and vegetables she wished and even a little bit of weed in the greenhouse she had built herself during her first week there. All of that for half the price she would pay to rent a bedsit in the city.

Rio loved Westview.

She had recently unpacked all her belongings: it had been five months. Her furniture was functional but not particularly tasteful, all surfaces were covered in plants.

There were no pictures on the walls, no knick-knacks anywhere, it was plain, plain and comfortable for her.

She now had twenty-four hours of free time before her unsurmountable nightshift on Friday. She sighed in anticipation: she really wished Agatha was going to behave.

Rio busied herself easily: working in the yard, tidying her bedroom, grocery shopping, laundry… And maybe, just maybe, she opened the spellbook she had borrowed from the library and looked for a spell to cast and cause general unpleasantness.

Not that she had anyone special in mind – even if she did, she was lacking the essential supplies anyway.

Friday night arrived much too quickly for her liking and with it, the inevitable call at midnight: disorderly conduct in a bar. The owner did not bother mentioning the name of the patron causing trouble.

Rio grabbed her utility belt and followed the officer she had been paired with for the night: Alice Wu Gulliver, her favourite.

Alice was sweet, a bit of a disaster but Rio could not really judge anyone’s professionalism. Most of the time, she did not care what crime had been committed: she arrested people because it was her job, not because she found it in anyway morally reprehensible.

The bane of Vidal’s existence was sitting outside the local bar, seemingly restrained by the owner.

“Okay, what did she do now?” Rio asked, slamming the door behind her.

Agatha’s eyes were red and swollen, she had a glass in her hand – the officer assumed she was both high and inebriated.

“She pickpocketed some guy.” The owner replied.

“I did not.” Agatha protested. She was not even trying to sound convincing.

“Open your purse, Agatha.” The owner said.

“I told you, you can’t make me.” She said petulantly.

“Sure I can’t, but they can.” He pointed at the two officers and walked back inside. Not his circus, not his monkeys.

The potential victim of theft left the bar to join the officers and give a statement. He had felt Agatha reach into his pockets and his wallet was coincidentally nowhere to be found since.

“Open your purse, Agatha.” Alice ordered.

“No way.” She said.

“We only have to arrest you to legally look inside, you know?” Rio reminded her to no avail.

The smirk on Agatha’s lips was almost carnivorous. “Fine, go ahead and cuff me, daddy.” She replied, holding her joint hands towards Vidal.

The officers sighed, Alice dealt with the handcuffing and Rio grabbed the purse. She opened it to find nothing but Agatha’s phone, a credit card in her name and a tube of lipstick.

“There’s nothing there.” Rio realised.

“What? Are you kidding?” Alice frowned and checked for herself.

“Just frisk her, it might be in her pockets or something.” She replied.

Agatha remained still, smiling smugly as Alice looked for the stolen wallet everywhere on her person.

“You should try looking up my vagina.” She snorted.

Rio rubbed her face in annoyance.

“Seriously? Did you even take anything?” Gulliver asked.

“Your mother’s butthole virginity.” She replied.

Rio just stared.

“I don’t care that you’ve allegedly fucked my mother, Agatha.” Alice merely rolled her eyes.

“She what?” Rio asked.

“It was a fun day.” Agatha told her with a wide smile.

“We should just let her go, Rio. She’s just being annoying.”

“Please, do.” Harkness cackled.

“No, it looks like she’s on a roll. She’ll make our shift hell if we don’t get her into custody. We can keep her in for drunkenness. And I’m pretty sure she’s high.” Rio noted. Her eyes were quite bloodshot.

Alice appeared reluctant, which was strange for her, she never minded taking suspects into custody. “Fine.” She finally said.

“I’ll consent to a cavity search if you’re the one performing it, handsome.” Agatha told Rio when she grabbed her arm to get her on the backseat.

“Jesus.” Rio whispered. Another terribly long night for her.

They drove in silence. In the rearview mirror, Vidal could see the smugness on Agatha’s face turn into something unreadable.

“Alright, Agatha, how about we drive you home?” Alice said after a moment of silence. They were nearing the city centre, only a few streets away from Agatha’s home.

“What?” The wannabe criminal asked. She frowned, upset by the very idea.

“Why?” Rio questioned next.

“Come on, you should spend today at home, not in a cell.” Alice said. Her tone was full of compassion for a reason unknown only to Rio.

Agatha bit the inside of her cheek. “No way, if you drive me home, I’ll just do worse. Want to see me destroy that bitch Hart’s petunias?” She threatened.

“Who’s Hart?” Rio asked.

“Mrs Davis.” Alice replied.

“She has azaleas.” Vidal corrected. She had complimented the old woman on her flowers several times.

“Okay. Nerd.” Agatha snorted.

“Agatha you’re one to talk. You used to be a teacher.” Alice replied.

“You did?” Rio was once again bewildered by Westview’s worst citizen.

“Yeah, she did.” Gulliver nodded.

“Damn, what happened? Did you show up drunk?” Rio joked.

Agatha looked offended. “Yeah, sure. Something like that.” She mumbled, looking away quickly.

The look on Alice’s face was enough for Rio to realise it may have been an insensitive joke to make. She continued to drive in silence.

Agatha knew the drill: she allowed them to take her shoes, her personal belongings and her jewellery – everything except her necklace which she was allowed to keep, Rio had realised the first time she had arrested her (Agatha had lost her mind at the mere suggestion of her taking it off for the night, a superior officer had to intervene and tell Rio to leave it with her).

Alice refused to take care of the paperwork, the drug test and the breathalyser, not wanting to hear another joke about Agatha having sex with her deceased mother, so Rio took care of it.

She stared at the negative test and ran it three more times. When the breathalyser told her Agatha could not have had more than two drinks, she turned to her.

“You’re not actually high. You’re not even drunk. Why won’t you go home?” She asked.

“I don’t feel like it.” Agatha replied. Her tone lacked its usual snarkiness, she would not even meet her eyes.

Rio was puzzled.

“Can you walk me to the cell now, officer?” She asked after a long silence.

“Right.” She nodded and led her to her resting place: the good old cell. Agatha stretched out on the cold hard bench and stared at the ceiling.

“Okay… Yell if you need anything.” Rio called.

She frowned when she was met with silence. No witty retort, no flirty comment, no nothing.

She sat at her desk, staring at the paperwork she was meant to fill: the suspect was accused of pickpocketing but no proof of theft, the suspect was taken in custody for disorderly conduct under the influence but she was neither inebriated nor on drugs, the suspect claims she will commit more crimes if left to her own devices but appears to be in a catatonic state.

Rio put down her pen and stared at the cell a while longer. There was something terribly wrong with Agatha, something she could not quite understand. She was used to the loud, obnoxious Agatha, furious Agatha, insane Agatha, rude Agatha… Not this Agatha who looked like an entirely different person.

A call from the owner of the bar informed her that the man’s wallet had been found on the floor.

Rio walked to the cell and unlocked the door. Her colleagues did not bother looking up from whatever they were doing; she had done so multiple times. She was a moth to flame, always finding a reason to talk to Harkness while she was in custody.

“You’d rather sleep in a cell than in a comfortable bed.” She said, sitting on the bench where Agatha laid.

“I didn’t want to go home today, okay? I can’t do it.” She replied. She sat up next to Rio, not even close enough to touch her which she would have usually done.

“Why not?” Rio pushed.

“Don’t act like you don’t know.” Agatha said.

The officer merely frowned. It seemed people in this town knew everything about everyone and expected her to do the very same after five small months. “I genuinely don’t know what you’re talking about.”

“Right…” Agatha stared at her, not sure whether to believe her. Rio’s doe eyes shone with sincerity and worry, it wasn’t the pity she was used to seeing in the rest of the town’s.

“Seven years ago today, I… lost my kid.” She admitted, looking down. She had not talked about it in so long. It hurt all the same, especially on the anniversary of his passing.

“Oh I’m… I’m so sorry.” Agatha could tell Rio wasn’t used to comforting people. She struggled to find the right words and she hesitantly reached for her arm in a gesture that was meant to be full of compassion but ended up being an awkward pat.

“What… How…? If you want to talk about it.” Rio dropped her hand back on her own lap. Idiot, she thought.

“He was six and he’s been dead longer than he’s been alive so that just…” Agatha bit the inside of her cheek. Speaking it aloud make it worse somehow. She felt like crying again – she hadn’t really stopped today.

“Sucks.” Rio completed for her.

She turned to the officer, ‘suck’, such a juvenile word from the mouth of a woman only ten years her junior. Rio was not well-spoken in emotional situations. “Sure.” She just said, snorting silently.

“Why don’t you go home, Agatha?” The officer asked.

“Because he’s everywhere.” Agatha sniffled. “Sometimes I just don’t want to be in there.”

Rio nodded and it seemed to click into place.

“So, you make sure you end up here?” She completed for Agatha.

“Here or at someone’s place.” Agatha shrugged. “Whatever, you’ve heard the rumours.” Her mask was back on, the sardonic town whore.

“I haven’t, actually.” Rio frowned.

She had seen first-hand what Agatha was capable of: the heavy make out sessions in the streets, the walks of shame on Sundays, the unnecessary flirting in line at the coffee shop; but she had not heard the rumours from the rest of Westview.

Agatha did not answer.

“Do you want to tell me how it happened?” Rio offered.

She wondered whether she should, whether she should put herself through the agony of speaking the words aloud and appear vulnerable in front of the one exciting thing this town had to offer. But Rio seemed so sincere, empathetic.

“It was my fault, I was careless. He was playing in the yard. I told him not to leave the property, you know? But he’s a kid so of course he didn’t listen.” She started. A tear rolled down her cheek, she wiped it, hoping the officer hadn’t seen it.

“I must have turned around for two minutes to get him a juice box and he sent his ball flying down the road, I guess, and ran to get it without me because ‘he’s a big boy now’, right?” She laughed mirthlessly. “A car hit him. It was going way too fast, he just… died on impact.” Her voice broke at the word and the sob she had been holding finally made it past her lips.

Rio grabbed her hand, it wasn’t anything she had learnt during her police training, not professional at all and she couldn’t care less. Agatha breathed in and scooted closer to rest her head on her shoulder.

“Did they find the driver?” Rio eventually asked.

“Oh yeah, he’s in prison but it kills me to know that he might be getting out in a couple of years.” She replied, wiping her nose with her sleeve.

Rio reached inside her pocket with her free hand to offer her a tissue and Agatha almost wished she had not. Being sexy was one thing, it could justify relentless pursuing and attention-seeking behaviour, but Rio being kind was another entirely, it unlocked Pandora’s box.

“I’m sorry. It’s unfair but it’s not your fault. You shouldn’t blame yourself. It was the driver’s responsibility.” Rio said.

“Yeah.” Agatha breathed in and removed her head from the officer’s shoulder lest she did something reckless like hug or thank her. “I guess I’ll just have to find him and kill him.” She joked.

“Right.” Rio said. She stared at Agatha, trying to figure out if there was any truth behind her statement.

“Anyway, here’s the sob story.” Harkness said. She rolled her shoulders and wiped the rest of her tears, her expression as smug as ever.

“Thanks for telling me.” Rio replied. She smiled weakly.

Agatha was relieved not to see pity painting her features, there was sympathy and something else, something close to affection. It looked terribly inviting.

And then, Rio stepped away without another word. Agatha tried to bury her disappointment deep inside, storing it away with the rest of her emotions. Rio had a job to do, she knew that.

But then, all but five minutes later, she walked back in carrying a steaming mug.

“Here you go.” She said, holding it out for her. The Westview Police logo seemed to taunt her.

“Is that coffee?” Agatha asked, surprised.

“Hot chocolate.” The officer.

“Why?” She frowned.

“Because it’s comforting.” Rio replied. She sat back on the bench.

Agatha smiled. “Thank you.” She muttered. It was unnatural on her tongue. She did not thank people much.

“So, you were a teacher?” Rio asked her after a while, smiling with strange curiosity.

“Yeah.” Agatha nodded. She had quit after Nicky’s death. She just could not focus anymore, could not handle disruption without snarling and tearing teens to pieces or wishing to physically hurt them.

“What did you teach?”

“Literature in Westview High.” She shrugged.

“Were you a bitch with the students? Or were you the passionate one everybody had a crush on?” Rio smiled.

“A crush on? Am I starting to wear you out?” Agatha joked.

“Never, Harkness.” The officer rolled her eyes but the blush on her cheeks belied her lips.

“Never say never, officer.” Harkness promised. “And for your information, I was a fucking bitch. They called me the Wicked Witch of English Department.”

“I’m sure you loved that title.”

“I sure did.” She smiled.

Rio drove Agatha home the next morning at the end of her shift and could not quite stop smiling even as the older woman told her:

“Thanks for last night, darling. I must admit, I did expect our first walk of shame to be a bit different than this.”

She stared, biting her bottom lip, making sure Agatha unlocked her door safely before driving off. The Maximoff boy waved at her with a knowing look in his eyes. His smile was wide, his eyeliner smudged… It took everything in her not to flip him off.

Rio drove home and collapsed in her bed. She stared at the ceiling, grinning like an idiot.

She would now be haunted by the terrible dichotomy of Agatha Harkness: the devilish menace to peace and order had proven to be a sensitive English teacher turned librarian. The officer could not quite wrap her head around it.

Simple attraction she could handle, but whatever this was? Oh, she was in trouble.

Chapter 2

Chapter Notes

this is super fun to write so it will be longer than 4 chapters

Rio did sincerely believe that the open-heart conversation she had with Agatha would be a game- changer, that the petty crime and lively arrests would stop. In retrospect, the officer thought her past self quite naïve.

Of course, they didn’t.

It got worse, she realised. Much, much worse: Agatha was no longer hiding her true intentions – not that she ever had – she would let everyone know that every single problem she was causing was to get Rio to pay attention to her.

Her colleagues no longer bothered driving to Agatha. They merely told Rio to deal with it and shrugged when she complained.

The rest of Westview seemed to catch on too, addressing their concerns directly to her as if she were personally in charge of Agatha, like some sort of legal guardian.

Rio sighed. The supermarket’s head of security had called her ten minutes earlier: attempted theft. Always the same culprit.

She slammed her car door and walked to the large building. An employee led her to the security room where she found Agatha sitting at the table and two irritated guards.

“So what’s going on?” Rio asked.

“Hey, sexy.” The culprit waved and winked from her chair. She seemed pleased with her situation and looked infuriatingly attractive.

“Good afternoon, Agatha.” The officer rolled her eyes. “Why aren’t you at work?”

“Some fancy guy from the state is visiting so the mayor asked me not to be there.” Agatha beamed. Rio could hardly blame the mayor.

“She just walked out the store with a TV and insisted we call you.” The head security guard told her.

He opened a laptop and played her the footage: as described, Agatha was walking out of the supermarket with a comically large television in her arms. She was not even trying to hide or run, she was smiling even. Rio would bet that she would have waved at the guards and the cameras had she had a free hand.

“Right.” She rubbed her forehead. “Why would you do that?” She asked Agatha.

“I’ve been a bad girl, officer. You must arrest me.” The woman replied in a breathy voice.

Rio exhaled in frustration. Of course, she would make a show of stealing a television just to flirt with her.

“This is a waste of my time. You obviously weren’t going to steal it. This is plain attention- seeking.” She replied curtly.

“Well, yeah. How else would I get your attention?” Agatha shrugged.

“Go home, Agatha.” Rio rolled her eyes before turning to the guards. “And you can just blacklist her, I’m not wasting police resources on this.”

The officer walked out of the room to leave the shop; she could hear Agatha hurrying to catch up with her.

“Can you drive me home? I walked here.” The woman asked.

“I’m not your personal taxi service.” Rio groaned.

Agatha seemed to be struggling to carry her handbag as if it were heavier than usual.

“So rude. Fuck the police.” She replied.

Rio just snorted. Another attempt at getting her angry. “You wish.” She replied.

“Well, can I?” Agatha asked.

“Not happening, Agatha.” She said dismissively. Not that she was personally against it, really. It would just not be professional. Plus, wouldn’t it be rewarding bad behaviour?

“You know the TV thing was actually my fifth attempt at getting the guards’ attention.” The woman started.

Rio truly tried to ignore her. She knew she was being baited. But she could not help it. “What?”

“I walked out of there with a lot of shit, and they never stopped me.”

“Sure.” She rolled her eyes.

“I’m not lying.” Agatha insisted. “Check my bag.”

“No, I won’t check your bag, Agatha.” Rio replied, she fished her keys out of her pocket and unlocked her car.

“Fine, your loss. I’m leaving with the goods.” The older woman shrugged.

“Enjoy your day off, Harkness.” She mumbled, relieved to finally be rid of the overzealous criminal.

“Hey, are you sure you can’t give me a ride to my place? These shoes are killing me.” Agatha insisted. She pointed to her burgundy high-heeled pumps. They were probably designer, a brand she should not have been able to afford on her librarian salary. “Don’t get me wrong, they make me look hot, but they are not comfortable.”

Rio looked her up and down: the killer stilettos, the figure-hugging slacks, the blouse that was too unbuttoned for a trip to the supermarket. She had to give it to her, she did look… mouth-watering.

“Fine.” She said. Agatha all but jumped in.

Rio promised herself to ignore her. She was doing her a service out of the goodness of their heart, it had nothing to do with how much she wanted to take her up on her offer to ‘fuck the police’. She found the quickest route to Agatha’s street, refusing to spend one second more than necessary in an enclosed space with her. Who knew what she would do? Insult her and kiss her?

“This is an android cable, I have an iPhone. Do you want it?” Her passenger said, holding a brand- new Samsung charging cable still in its box.

“I have an iPhone too.” Rio mumbled, focusing on the road. How Agatha could have made the mistake she did not know.

“Do you want earphones? They’re old fashioned with a wire and everything.” She waved another unopened box at Rio’s face.

“Nope.” The officer rolled her eyes and continued to ignore her.

She could hear her fumbling in her bag, cardboard against cardboard, metallic noises and something like liquid in a glass container, Rio guessed.

She frowned and turned to the older woman in the passenger seat.

“Did you steal all this?” She asked.

Agatha smiled triumphantly. “Did I?”

“What the fuck?” She hit the brakes a little too hard. The car behind her honked. “You need to bring it back to the store!”

Agatha did not answer. Rio kept driving to her street anyway, she needed her out of there as soon as possible.

“Do you want a phone case? It’s one those magsafe things.” She held out a brand-new apple product that Rio knew to cost at least fifty dollars. “I don’t need it.” She threw it in the backseat.

“They did NOT let you walk out with all this.” Rio said. “You have got to be kidding me.”

Saved by the bell: the car pulled into Agatha’s neighbourhood. “Oh, this is my stop. Thanks for the ride, darling. You can keep the tequila.” She said with a wink.

Rio barely had time to register her words that the door was slammed in her face.

“What tequila?” She asked, more confused than ever. And then she spotted it, a bottle of tequila in the passenger seat. A very nice one.

She just stared, attempting to process the rollercoaster Agatha had put her through. That woman had managed to get stopped by security guards for stealing a television while having hundreds of dollars’ worth of goods in her handbag, admitting to it while still in the shop just to be rebuked by a police officer…

Rio sighed and sunk in her seat. Was she supposed to arrest Agatha after purposefully letting her walk free? She breathed out and eyed the phone case in the backseat: it was green, her favourite colour, and just the right kind for her phone.

“Fuck that shit.” She decided. If the security guards had not caught Agatha, it was their problem. She drove away without looking back.

Idiot. Idiot. Idiot. Idiot.

She tried to ignore the ache between her legs. She should go to church, she thought, to purify her mind from evil thoughts. (She had never once attended a service in her entire life)

“Baby, I don’t smoke weed.” The librarian said.

“I’ve literally arrested you when you were high.” She snorted.

“That’s not possible because I don’t do marijuana.” Agatha sounded adamant; she was such a good liar Rio did not know what to believe.

“Yeah right.” She rolled her eyes. “That’s such an asshole move, Agatha. You can buy weed anywhere. You could have literally asked me for some, I would have given it to you.”

She had a boxful, more than twice what she would need for the season. Her plants were always lush and fruitful.

“You shouldn’t have messed with the Chart.” Agatha said.

Rio did not hide her frustrated growl. “We are not dating; you can’t keep me from other women.”

“You are so wrong about that.” The librarian replied.

“I will fuck a man. I don’t care. If women won’t touch me, I will get with a guy.”

“In fact, my neighbour to the left, a hot divorcee has been flirting with me. I’ll go over right now and sleep with him. I’ll stay on the phone and everything.”

“Go ahead, I’d love to hear it.” The other woman replied.

“I hate you.” She groaned.

“This isn’t the pharmacy, Joe.” The librarian replied curtly. “Don’t be a stranger, babygirl.” She cooed.

“Fuck you.” Rio said but Agatha had already hung up.

She giggled. He looked too adorable for her to be angry.

“Very cute.” She typed. “When can I get my shovel back?”

“It’s under your hedge, your other neighbour Marigold’s bushes.” Agatha sent back.

“Are they drunk?” Alice asked.

Rio looked closer: wide pupils, bloodshot eyes, hilarity. “I think they’re high.”

A plate full of crumbs laid on the table in the back of the room.

“Hey there youngsters.” Rio greeted them with a saccharine smile. “Are we having a party?” They all burst laughing.

“Hello, Officer!” A lady replied. “We’re having a really good session.”

“I’ll bet.” Rio said. “So what’s going on? Did you guys do anything before coming here?”

“Oh no, it’s business as usual.”

“Right.”

“Your girlfriend made us a delicious batch of cookies; it’s put us in such high spirits.”

“My girlfriend?” She asked through gritted teeth.

“She surprised us with them when we got there. You’re really softening her up, officer.” A few other elderly people nodded along.

“I just unlock the door for them, I don’t care what they do in there.” Agatha shrugged.

“I brought cookies? That’s so generous of me.” She giggled.

Vidal’s sigh fuelled Agatha’s satisfaction.

“Oh please, I assume they’re wearing diapers.” The librarian snorted.

“I can’t believe this.” Rio whined, rubbing her face.

“Did you use the weed you stole from my greenhouse?” The officer whispered.

The other woman leaned in to ask: “Would you punish me if I did?”

She used the Harkness Special on her target, the one that could fluster anyone: she remained close, biting her lips and looking at her lips and eyes alternately with a half-lidded gaze.

“She’s a liar and a bitch.” Rio replied curtly.

“Business as usual then.”

Her audience grew comically concerned, they gasped dramatically, bringing hands to their faces and dragging their fingers down to stretch their skin.

“Wrong?” One of them asked.

“Are we going to die?” Another replied.

“Were they poisoned?” A man gasped.

“I think my skin is melting off.” A woman whined.

Rio hid her amusement with impressive professionalism. “Not poisoned per se, just laced with cannabis.” She announced.

“Really? They tasted delicious.” Someone replied.

“I’m sure they did.” Alice rolled her eyes.

“I feel great. I don’t think it’s affecting me at all.” A man said.

“Why are you holding your leg up?” The young woman asked.

“It will fall if I don’t.” He replied.

Rio did not grace that with an answer.

“So, listen, we can’t let you drive home or leave you alone in any way, you guys need to be supervised but we don’t want to transport you to the station because you didn’t take the weed willingly, you shouldn’t be in a cell. The officers were surprised to see them nod in acceptance. She breathed out in relief. It might be a smoother operation than anticipated.

“That sounds fun. Can we get a little music, some snacks, drinks…?” A woman asked.

“You can get snacks and non-alcoholic beverages, but I doubt Agatha will allow you to play music in here.” Rio answered.

She turned to Alice. “By the way, can you update her on the situation? Tell her to close to the public and to get the fuck out, I don’t want to see her hanging around here.”

“Can’t you do it?” Gulliver asked. “You know she likes to go through you for all police matters.”

“Agatha.” She called.

The librarian looked up and frowned. “Where’s the hot one?”

“Don’t start with me, Harkness. What you did is seriously fucked up.” Alice said.

“Do not use that tone with me, Gulliver.” It was Miss Harkness, the English teacher in charge of the Introduction to American Prose module who addressed her. “I haven’t forgotten that pathetic excuse for an essay about Hawthorne you handed during Junior Year. You couldn’t even spell his name and now you’re trying to scold me.”

Alice cowered away, her authority and confidence vanishing. “Oh, come on, that was twelve years ago!”

“What do you want?” Agatha asked.

“We’re keeping them here to contain the situation. You need to close to the public.”

“Amazing.” She beamed at the prospect of going home early and not being forced to interact politely with people.

“Rio wants you out as soon as possible.” Alice added.

“Does she?” Agatha’s smirk grew more mischievous.

“Yes, she was adamant.” The officer shrugged.

“The lady doth protest too much, methinks.” Harkness simply said.

“Whatever.” Alice rolled her eyes.

She could only watch as Agatha stepped out from behind the counter to walk towards the Book Club room.

“Hey, baby. I hear you’re holding these fine people hostage?” The librarian said obnoxiously loudly for everyone to hear.

Rio glared. “Don’t.”

It did not deter Agatha from taking the empty seat next to her. Let’s give Westview’s elderly a show, she decided, feeling all eyes on them.

“Are we having a party?” She asked.

“You’re going home.” Rio fired back.

“I could stay and help.” She smiled.

The officer scoffed. “You? Help? You would cause more trouble.”

“That is not true, I am an upstanding member of the community.” She said smugly.

“Go away.” Rio replied. There was no bite to her words, she rolled her eyes, amused by her antics.

“You break my heart, darling.” Agatha pouted.

“This is the person who fed you space cookies, the whole reason why you are in this state and why you can’t go home. You were drugged against your will, you can and should press charges. Miss Harkness has committed a federal offense. Who wants to make an official statement?” Rio told the room. She smirked, eying the librarian with spite. She would get her charged. She was determined.

Agatha was not worried; she looked around the room. Her victims did not seem violated, and if they had, she would have simply intimidated them. Threatening to stop housing the Book Club would be a perfect way to do so.

No one raised a hand. Rio frowned, narrowing her eyes.

“Awkward. Let’s all forget this happened.” Agatha taunted her.

“Get out of here, Agatha.” The officer scoffed.

“Alright.” She giggled. “I’ll gather my things and leave you to your babysitting duties.”

“You’ll pay for this.” Rio mumbled.

“I sure hope I do.” An opportunity Agatha could not miss. She walked out of the room but turned around before disappearing from view. “Want to say hello to Senor Scratchy?”

Rio’s nostrils flared. It was yet another excuse to get her alone, she knew it. “I do, actually.” She stood and joined Agatha. She did want to cuddle her rabbit. No euphemism here.

Alice took over her duties while she followed the librarian to the counter. Scratchy was sitting in his cage and welcomed the opportunity to receive attention.

“You know you’re an asshole, right?” Rio said, cradling him against her chest.

Agatha’s smile was genuine, not smug, not wicked. “Oh, come on, they’re having fun. This might be the nicest thing I’ve ever done in my life.” She laughed.

“You’ve drugged people against their will.” The officer reminded her.

“Stop being so dramatic. I gave some lonely people a bit of weed and now they’re having a party. It’s probably helped their joint health and kept them from sitting at home alone and being miserable.” Agatha gathered her belongings, turned off her computer and cleaned up her desk.

“Why?” Rio asked. She doubted Agatha truly cared about the elderly.

“It got you here, didn’t it?” She replied sincerely.

There was no mischief behind her eyes, just honesty. She appeared in front of the officer like the subdued version of herself, one that seemed true, the same one she had encountered on the anniversary of the death of her son.

She looked away from the librarian’s intense blue eyes. Moments like these made her think Agatha wanted more with her, more than just a one-night stand, more than just sex, more than a challenge. Her idiotic heart started beating out of control. Her skin longed for Agatha’s touch.

She buried her face deeper inside Scratchy’s fur.

The idea that Agatha felt anything more for her than mere attraction was incompatible with everything else she knew about her. She could not understand how it all fit together into one person.

Agatha the player, the scammer, the petty criminal, and sensitive Agatha, reasonable Agatha, cultured Agatha who texted her covers of books about gardening and witchcraft that she might like. The Agatha who infuriated her and the Agatha who made her stomach flutter.

Rio wondered if the librarian could feel the awkward tension growing by the second between them. Anger and frustration had given way to something different, something too real triggered by Agatha’s honesty.

“May I have my rabbit back so I can go home?” She eventually asked.

“You don’t deserve to have him back.” Rio narrowed her eyes in her direction but it lacked her usual bite.

Agatha smiled sweetly and grabbed Senor Scratchy to secure him in his carrier. She put on her coat, put her bag over her shoulder and grabbed her rabbit with one hand.

It was a strange sight, one that did not align with Agatha’s unhinged behaviour and stupid ideas. Another physical representation of Soft Agatha. Rio stared at her with wide-eyed wonder. If only she weren’t so saturnian. If only she weren’t the most dangerous predator in Westview.

The librarian stopped in front of her, close enough for Rio to feel her warmth but far enough for their bodies not to touch. With her free hand, she grabbed the officer’s face and kissed her cheek.

“Bye, Rio.” She said before leaving the library entirely.

Rio stood there for a while, her face and neck burning red. She fought the urge to smile and giggle and simply melt. She felt like a teenager, she wanted to lay down on a bed and call her best friend to tell her all about the interaction while kicking her feet in the air and twirling the phone cord. She breathed in and out to still her pounding heart and chase the blood away from her face and neck.

Rio walked back into the room once she felt in appropriate spirits. She was met with giggles and whispers. Alice handed her a tissue.

“You have lipstick on your face.” She said.

Rio’s blood rushed to her cheeks once more. It provided entertainment for the rest of the afternoon and long into the evening.

Rio walked into the library for the second time that Tuesday. She was out of uniform, off service and free as a bird. Her resolution to stay away from Agatha had slowly disintegrated, though she would never admit it to the she-devil herself. The entire town already believed they were dating; the older woman was relentless. What harm could it do? Perhaps spending time with her would raise enough red flags for Rio to learn her lesson.

“Hey, Agatha. I’d like to return these books.” She said once she reached the counter.

Agatha did not answer. In fact, she appeared entirely unresponsive for an extended period of time. Were it not for the way her eyes were travelling up and down Rio’s body, the officer might have suspected a stroke or a ruptured aneurism.

Rio bit back a smile. She may have dressed up knowing she would be running into Agatha that day (going to the library with the excuse of returning books she had not even had time to read). Crop tops and tight jeans were a staple in her wardrobe – something conservative members of the community seemed to disapprove of if she believed the glares she received at the supermarket – but that day, she had picked an especially tight top and leather slim cut trousers.

Agatha was not hiding her appreciation for it all: the bare abs, the shape of her nipples visible through the thin fabric of her top…

“Give a girl a warning next time, Rio.” She finally said, making a show of fanning herself with a stack of papers from her desk. “I’m ovulating, you’re killing me.”

Rio looked down to hide her smile and her blush. “You still ovulate?” She asked, teasing.

She set the books down on the counter and leaned against it.

“Fuck you, officer. I will find your mother and I will fuck her.” The librarian replied.

“Well, if you find her, please tell her to contact me because I haven’t been able to track her down since she dropped me off at the fire station when I was two weeks old.” Rio deadpanned.

Agatha’s face fell. “What? I’m so sorry.”

The officer burst out laughing.

“Seriously?” The older woman sighed.

“I’m kidding.” Rio giggled and then abruptly stopped. “She’s dead.”

Agatha stared. “Is this another joke?”

“No, she is very dead.” She nodded.

“I’m sorry.” The librarian said hesitantly.

Rio just brushed her off with a wave of her hand.

“Anyway, I stopped by this morning and that gay kid was in charge apparently.”

“Billy?” Agatha asked.

Rio shrugged, uninterested in learning about the teenager. “Where were you?”

“I was running an errand.” The librarian said, narrowing her eyes.

“What sort of errand?”

“A personal errand.” The question upset her. It was none of her business, she didn’t owe her accountability just because they texted every day and she was trying to sleep with her.

“Okay…” Rio replied rolling her eyes. She was certain it was another illegal endeavour of Agatha’s.

“Billy volunteers here all the time, he knows how to work the computer. You could have left the books with him.” The older woman said.

“I don’t like talking to kids.” She replied.

Agatha snorted. Books to be returned could be dropped off in a box outside the library. Rio would not have needed to interact with Billy at all. “Just say you wanted to see me, you don’t need an excuse.”

“No, I needed to drop these books off, you just happen to be the librarian.” The officer lied.

“Whatever you say.” Agatha shrugged. It was still a small victory. “Don’t just stand there, come here and sit.” She patted the seat next to her behind the counter.

“Aren’t you supposed to be working?”

“I can multitask. I’m good with my fingers.” She winked.

“Oh, come on.” Rio sighed. The irritating, over the top flirting.

“The Book Club came back to see me and asked for the name of my weed supplier.” Agatha said.

“Please, tell me you did not say it was me.” Rio flumped down on the officer chair and started swivelling from left to right causing it to squeak lightly.

“I did, I gave them your address and the key to your greenhouse.” Agatha smiled.

“That’s not funny. I could have you arrested.”

“Yeah, right.” Agatha snorted.

She looked down at Rio’s chair and stopped it from moving with one hand. She did not much like noise. “Stop that.” She ordered.

The other woman obeyed without resistance, unconsciously sitting straighter. Something about Agatha giving her orders made her stomach flutter.

“Your reign of impunity will come to an end.” She mumbled.

“Of course it will.” Agatha just giggled. “You want to see Senor Scratchy?”

She did not wait for an answer and just released the rabbit.

“He is the reason I came at all.” Rio let him sit in her lap, gently touching his fur.

“Sure, and you dressed like a slutty teenager on Halloween for him too.”

“I did not!” The younger woman looked down to hide her blush. “This is age appropriate.”

“’Sup Agatha.” He mumbled.

“Your hairdresser’s parole officer, he got out of jail last week and he’s taking clients again so go for it.” Rio replied curtly. She glared at him unblinkingly.

“I’ve got an assignment to do. It’s poetry analysis, I hate it.” He said.

“I didn’t know you could read.” The librarian grinned. Rio snickered next to her.

“That’s funny.” He replied mirthlessly.

“Which poet?” She asked.

“TS Eliot.”

“Piece of cake. You need Mastering American Literature; it’s in section twelve. Super old but reliable.” She said.

“Can’t you help me?” Tommy asked. He pouted slightly, it was a dance he was familiar with.

“Okay…” He frowned.

“All you need to do is ask for the person’s card and scan it.” She grabbed the scanner to show him. “Then you take the books and scan them one by one. If someone is dropping off a book, just leave it on the desk and if the phone rings, you don’t answer. Got it?”

“Is that what you do all day? I could do your job.” Tommy snorted.

“That kid’s put me in a bad mood.” Rio replied petulantly. She tightened the scarf around her and breathed in Agatha’s perfume. It was intoxicating, probably one of these custom-made scents that were created to communicate the depth of one’s character. It was powerful, addictive, irresistible.

“I found him quite pleasant today, he always is when he has a favour to ask.” Agatha smiled. She held the door open for Rio.

“Something about him rubs me off the wrong way, I just don’t like him.” The younger woman said.

“He’s Billy’s twin brother.”

She rolled her eyes. “It must run in the family then.”

Rio let her buy her coffee and a pastry, still wearing her scarf and she sat facing her on a table for two. Her heart skipped a few beats as she watched Agatha laugh and talk animatedly.

It felt like a date. She chased away the thought as quickly as it had come. Not a date. Just coffee between two women who happened to be very attracted to one another.

“I don’t know how you survived teaching. I couldn’t deal with teenagers all day.” Rio said.

“I’d take teenagers over adults any day.” Agatha replied. “But I must admit, towards the end, there were close calls. I removed myself from several situations to avoid strangling kids.”

“One time, a senior who had just transferred from another school in the district tried my patience, I guess the others did not warn him about me. “I saw you hiss at Tommy, and Billy said you called him Gerard Way this morning.” Agatha replied with a wide smile.

“I don’t know how to interact with children.” The officer frowned. She was only slightly embarrassed.

“So, you just antagonise them? You’re like a chihuahua.” The older woman joked.

She burst out laughing when Rio bared her teeth at her and started growling.

They finished their coffees and pastries fast but stayed a while longer to finish their conversation. Agatha’s cheeks hurt from laughing and Rio could not look away from the way her blue eyes crinkled when she smiled.

There was genuine Agatha again, the one with whom she could have a conversation without having to arrest her. The real Agatha, not the performer.

“Oh no, I need to get back.” The librarian realised. It had been longer than twenty minutes, much much longer.

She held her arm as they hurried across the street to reach her building of employment. Thankfully, her superior, the mayor herself, never stopped by the library. She tried to avoid Agatha as much as she could.

“Oh, they’re multiplying.” Rio muttered in disgust as they arrived. Billy was sitting behind the counter with Tommy.

“Well, if it isn’t Chip and Dale.” Agatha smiled.

“You said twenty minutes.” Tommy complained. “That’s unpaid and coerced child labour.”

“I can’t deal with this.” The officer said. She rolled her eyes. “I’m going home.”

Agatha concealed her disappointment. “Okay. It was good to see you.” She smiled genuinely.

“You too.” Rio said. She smiled back, not bothering to hide it. She tried to remove the scarf to hand it to its rightful owner.

“Keep it, I wouldn’t want Westview’s finest officer to get cold on her way to her car.” The librarian made a show of adjusting the scarf back around her to keep her warm.

“Thank you.” Rio beamed. “I’ll give it back as soon as I can.”

“We should do this again.” Agatha said, her hand still holding the scarf. Her voice was soft, almost shy. Her own offer surprised her. It felt awfully close to asking her on a date, something she had not done with another woman in almost two decades.

Rio stared for a moment, wondering if it was genuine or just another way to tease her.

As she was met with silence, Agatha’s demeanour changed back to her usual Performer persona. “Preferably without clothes on, though that doesn’t seem to be a problem for you.” She winked.

Rio looked away. The fire ignited by the other woman faltered, almost drowning in her disappointment. Perhaps it had all been in her head, the genuine connection. Perhaps this was nothing more than an elaborate plan of Agatha’s to sleep with her and discard her the next morning. “Bye, Agatha.” She said as she stepped out of the building.

The librarian sighed, knowing she had said the wrong thing, yet again.

“Where have you been?” Tommy asked. She waved him out of her chair. “You were gone for over an hour.”

“We were at the café.” She shrugged in her seat.

“That’s so cute.” Billy beamed.

“Shut up, Billiam.” Agatha rolled her eyes.

“Are you blushing?” Tommy said just to taunt her.

She would never admit to that, especially not in front of these two teenagers. “Fuck off, virgins. Do you want my help or not?”

“Yes, please, I’m desperate.” He reached for his bag to show her the assignment.

Trouble found Rio in the form of a text at the beginning of her nightshift that Friday. It was from Agatha like most of the messages she received were these days.
