The officer shook her head and fished a ten dollar note out of her wallet to hand to the librarian.

“Thank you. Goodbye.” The older woman said. She then stood up from her chair, turned around and put the note away in her bra.

“I saw you pocket that. I’ll tell your supervisor.” Rio threatened.

“Snitches get stitches.” Agatha mumbled. She turned back to face her. “You’re holding up the line.”

“There’s no line.” Rio said. The library was completely empty.

Agatha had already grabbed her phone and started scrolling, refusing to give the officer any more attention.

“For what it’s worth, I’m sorry about Friday.” Rio told her.

The librarian lifted her perfectly manicured left middle-finger in response, not even looking at her. She agreed to leave and count her losses. She supposed she had deserved that.

Rio did not overreact. Rio did not wallow in an infinity pool of sense pity. Rio did not feel smothered by a crippling sense of sadness and loneliness that could only be cured by Agatha.

She was very reasonable about it. She did not go home and lay in bed holding the scarf she had never given Agatha back.

She did not wake up the next morning and decide to ‘accidentally’ run into Agatha again to have a chance to talk it all over. If the librarian would just hear her out, it would solve all their issues.

She found her at the coffee shop where she usually stopped to take her break when it was too cold to sit and chain-smoke on the bench in front of the library.

She was sitting at a table nursing a coffee and typing on her phone. Rio was always fascinated by the number of texts and emails she seemed to send every day. Who knew what they were about. It couldn’t all be blackmail or threats; she doubted Agatha left a paper-trail anywhere.

“Agatha, hi! I’m so glad I caught you.” Rio said, sitting down.

She saw Agatha’s face twist in anger before turn back to her focused expression.

“I’m busy.” She replied without looking away from her phone.

“You’re alone drinking coffee.” Rio received no reply, she sat on the opposite chair anyway. “Look, Agatha, I really need to apologise, I shouldn’t have said what I said, I didn’t mean it and I shouldn’t have done what I did on Friday. I’m so sorry.”

The other woman continued to type furiously on her screen. Her eyebrows twitched and that was the only sign that she had heard what Rio had said. “I’m meeting someone and you’re scaring the bitches away.” She said. She dismissed her with a wave of her hand.

“You shouldn’t talk about women like that.” Rio replied.

She stood up, anyway. Angering Agatha would lead to nowhere.

“Eat my ass.” The woman said curtly as she left.

Rio realised how inappropriate it would be for her to answer ‘I wish’, so she kept her mouth shut and left the coffee shop.

Rio had to be a glutton for punishment. Or simply an idiot. Or just unable to read the room. She walked into the library the next day and made a beeline for the shelves, avoiding being seen by Agatha. She grabbed two books from the Wiccan section without looking at the titles and walked to the counter.

“I’d like to borrow these.” She said. The librarian was engrossed by whatever it was she was doing on her computer. She looked up at Rio and her nostrils flared in anger.

“Fine.” She replied curtly. She grabbed the books and the library card from her hand in an almost violent gesture and began to scan them.

“Can we talk, Agatha?” Rio asked.

“No, fuck you.” Agatha replied without looking at her.

She threw the books back at her and swivelled in her chair to focus on her computer screen.

Rio sighed. “Okay. Have a good day.”

“Kill yourself.” The librarian said.

“Right.” She sighed again and left the building. It was clear Agatha was no closer to forgiving her. She would have to try another method.

The next day, she walked into the coffee shop again and tried her hand at bribing the librarian during her break.

“Can you bring the muffin to Agatha?” She asked after ordering her own coffee.

“Alright.” The barista said. She looked like she wanted to roll her eyes but walked to Agatha’s table anyway.

“She said you need to leave her alone.” She told Rio as she made her way back behind the counter.

“Oh.” The officer pouted.

“But she did take the muffin.”

“Cool.” She shrugged and grabbed her cup. “Thanks, have a good day.”

“Hey, if you ever get over her, I could give you my number.” The young woman smiled. It was bold of her to say these words while Agatha was within earshot. But Agatha did not react, she did not threaten her, she did not glare, she did not start researching the young woman to find the best way to cause her harm.

She just let her proposition the person she used to guard fiercely.

“Oh…” Rio looked towards Agatha. “I don’t…” She tried to deny it but realised the barista had been privy to many of her encounters with the librarian.

“Hey, it’s fine, officer. Have a good day too.” She smiled and let Rio leave without having to come up with an excuse or a convincing lie.

She was one of many women who had made their interest known. Agatha had officially lifted the embargo on her, the thought was devastating. She missed belonging to her. She missed her possessive fury and confident ownership.

She missed everything that she had once found infuriating about Agatha.

Rio did not realise that her next encounter with Agatha would be through the one teenager in town she could not stand: Billy who she had pulled over and caught with a bottle of vodka and a few grams of cannabis in the passenger seat of his car on a Friday night.

He had to be the biggest idiot in town, she thought, driving around without at least putting everything away. No one would have stopped him had he not left it all out in the open for them to see.

Billy had officially been arrested and Rio almost felt pity at the way his tears were smudging his eyeliner.

“You’re underage, I need to call your parents.” She said.

He sat in the chair next to her desk and sobbed as she wrote her report and asked him standard questions. “Please, can I call someone else? Another guardian? It’s a misunderstanding, I swear.”

She rolled her eyes and agreed. The thought of having to speak to his mother made her sick. She hated the woman. And his bland father seemed too uninteresting and boring for her to handle. Let him call anyone else.

It did not occur to her that the other adult he could call would be someone very familiar to her, not before the person in question strutted into the police station with a malevolent sneer on her face. She was wearing a pantsuit and holding a designer handbag that screamed “I have a very good lawyer.”

“Agatha?” Rio called as she walked to her desk.

“Whatever happened, he didn’t do it.” The older woman replied. She stepped in front of Billy to shield him for the officer.

“We found alcohol and weed in his car.” Rio said.

“It wasn’t his.” There was no hesitation in her tone.

“It was in his car.”

“It was mine.” Agatha said.

Rio rolled her eyes. “Why would your alcohol and weed be in his car? You don’t even do weed.”

“He helped me out, I didn’t feel like leaving the house, he was bringing them over.” Agatha crossed her arms. She looked at the boy pointedly, making sure he went along with her version of events.

Rio did not bother mentioning Billy was driving away from his street to a destination on the other side of town as Agatha’s own home.

“Yes, it’s hers.” The teenager confirmed, nodding vigorously.

“I know you’re lying.” Rio huffed.

“Am I?” Agatha started smiling, a dangerous smile that did nothing to conceal the scalding fury in her eyes. “Doesn’t that fit the image you have of me, huh, Rio? A drugged up alcoholic mess? Surely, I’d be the kind to ask a kid to bring me weed and alcohol, wouldn’t I?”

The officer sighed. “Agatha…”

“Just test him for alcohol and drugs. That boy is a fucking nerd, he didn’t take anything.” She added.

Rio breathed out and looked at the two of them. Agatha’s protective stance over Billy made it impossible for her to refuse. “We’re testing you.” She said. “One trace of anything and you’re being charged.”

“Okay.” He nodded.

Fifteen minutes later, Billy walked out free of charge.

“Agatha, wait.” She called before they could leave the station.

“What?” The other woman barked.

“Why would you help him?” Rio asked.

“Are you arresting me?” Agatha replied.

“No, if it is yours, it’s legal, we’re just confiscating it because you asked a kid to hold onto it. And for what it’s worth, leaving it in the passenger seat was a pretty dumb thing to do.”

“Goodbye, officer.” She said.

The door slammed behind her.

Rio’s first encounter with Business Agatha had gone… Terribly wrong. She understood why the entire town recoiled in horror at the idea of upsetting her now.

It dawned on her then that there was no fixing them anymore. Agatha did not want her to. The realisation hit her like a truck and sent her into a spiral of conflicting emotions from guilt to hatred to despair to pure agony.

Some mornings, she would wake up and feel so lonely without news of Agatha that she could hardly move or eat or be productive. Some nights, she would miss her so much she would start listing reasons why she hated her, why Agatha was a bitch and not a great loss. Some afternoons, she would hardly be able to resist the pull to walk into the library and the coffee shop just to see her, even if it meant starting a fight.

Rio had expected it, the heart-wrenching pain caused by Agatha’s withdrawal of attention. She had known it was coming, only under different circumstances; she had truly believed the woman would sleep with her and discard her the next morning and that it would end that way. It would have been easy to hate Agatha then, to blame her for everything, to paint her as the villain. Yet, here she was, blaming herself, wondering what she could have done differently to keep her near.

She could have refused to switch shift with Alice that Sunday.

She could have called Agatha an uber and gone to sleep.

She could have let her drive home at the end of the night.

She could have taken the couch.

She could have kept her face under the comforter and never kissed her.

She could have told her to stop without an explanation.

She could have gone to sleep without telling her to forget all about their kiss.

She could have kept her mouth shut and not called her a mess or an alcoholic or whatever else she had told her.

Rio wished she could go back in time and slap herself in the face.

Ever since that night she lived aimlessly. She realised how dramatic it sounded, she just could not help it.

Desperation had driven her into a corner: she had tried everything in her power to get Agatha back. Texting, calling, emailing, transferring dollars to her Venmo account to get her attention, visiting, leaving peace-offerings – and nothing worked.

And then, she had tried spells, silly spells, manifestation spells, spells to forget her, spells for Agatha to forgive her, spells to change the past… It was all over her: the paper in her phone case with Agatha’s name on it, the jar with her picture in her freezer, the cord cutting ritual that ended without the cord being cut – a smidge of hope destroyed by Agatha telling her to “kill herself” the next day.

It was worse than ever. None of her heartbreaks had ever left her so raw. None of them had deprived her of sleep or appetite or simply joy. Agatha Harkness was special that way.

Alice had reached her breaking point. It had been twenty-two days, twenty-two days of Rio moping around, being miserable and making it everyone’s problem. It was unbearable, being paired with her, which happened constantly these days. They were a good duo, according to their colleagues. Alice knew the real reason was that none of them wanted to be stuck with Rio for an entire shift.

The older woman would not talk about it, not even when pressed on the matter. She kept repeating that she was fine, that she felt great, that nothing was wrong. She was lying through her teeth and doing a terrible job at it. Alice was not that incompetent that she could not tell.

It was just after rush hour in Westview, the two of them were parked in a residential era, right after a stop sign, lurking for potential lawbreakers. Rio was sitting being the wheel looking as blasé as ever until a familiar Mercedes SUV drove past them. She perked up and drove to follow it.

“What are you doing?” Alice asked.

“She’s speeding.” Rio replied.

She was getting closer to the car, almost tailing her.

“No, you’re speeding to catch up with her.”

“No, see, she’s speeding.” Rio pointed at their speedometer; it showed them driving two miles per hour over the limit. “I’m right behind her; I’m going as fast as she is.”

Alice huffed. “That’s crazy. You can’t be crazier than the craziest bitch in town, Rio. This is getting fucking concerning.”

“She won’t be getting away with this.”

It was too late for Alice to stop her; Rio had already turned the lights and siren on to tell the driver to pull over. The Mercedes complied right away.

Sighing, Alice followed Rio out of the vehicle. She needed to make sure they avoided an unnecessary commotion. When they reached the driver, the window was already down.

“Do you know why I’ve arrested you?” Rio asked, her shoulders squared.

“Because you’re obsessed with me.” Agatha replied. She rolled her eyes and handed her both her licence and registration.

Alice was relieved to find her so calm. Let this be as quick a process as possible.

“You were speeding in a residential area.” Rio said.

Agatha looked at them both, one eyebrow raised. “Okay?” She was not. She knew she was not and the two cops did too.

“I thought you of all people would know better.” Rio told her, handing her the licence and registration back. She had not even bothered looking at them.

Alice gasped silently and watched the scene unfold. A commotion was inevitable, and she was not certain she wanted to defend Rio this time.

Agatha’s eyes flashed with fury, she threw her papers on the passenger seat and unbuckled her seatbelt in one swift movement. Rio took a step back, realising her mistake, she stared as the car door almost hit her. Then, Agatha jumped out, ready to pounce on her.

That was it, Alice thought. They were going to fight.

The librarian raised a clenched fist in the air, the one holding her keys and with two rings on her fingers.

This one would hurt, Alice cringed.

But before her fist could make impact with Rio’s face, Agatha stopped herself.

“No. Not worth it.” She said instead, glaring at the officer who was still frozen on the spot.

For good measure, she spat in her face, delighting in the way Rio’s eyes closed in shock and regret. Then she climbed back into her car.

“Fuck you. Mail me that speeding ticket and whatever else you’re charging me with, you evil bitch.” She said, driving away, almost hitting Rio in the process.

“Get in the fucking car.” Alice said through gritted teeth. “Give me the keys, I’m driving.”

Rio obliged and hurried to sit in the passenger seat, staring at her worn black Doc Martens. She grabbed a tissue to wipe Agatha’s spit off her face.

They drove in silence, Alice’s anger seeping out her body in waves that Rio could almost taste.

She knew she had messed up.

Alice parked the car on its designated spot at the station and turned to her.

“That was fucking cruel. I wish she had beaten you up. I would have let it happen.” She spat.

“I know.” Rio mumbled, still staring at her feet.

Alice could see how tense her jaw was, how she seemed to be biting the inside of her cheeks.

“I don’t know what happened between you two but that doesn’t give you the right to imply whatever you were implying.” She scolded.

It felt like yelling at a child, Rio was certainly acting like one, refusing to look at her.

“I know.”

“Her kid was hit by a car. Speeding is the one thing she doesn’t do around town.”

“I know.”

“You’re so fucking stupid.”

Rio finally snapped: “I know. I regretted it the moment I said it, okay?”

Alice breathed out. She did look regretful, on the verge of tears, fists clenched, and eyebrows knitted.

“You need to pull yourself together.” She said.

“I’m trying.”

“Try harder.” She pushed.

“I am.” Rio raised her voice and glared.

It satisfied Alice, seeing her emote, anything was better than Rio remaining closed off.

“What happened anyway?” She asked.

“Nothing.”

“Something obviously happened.”

“Nothing happened.” Rio kept denying.

The younger woman scoffed. “So, you went from fucking to hating each other for no reason?”

“We were never fucking.” She replied, confused.

“Yeah right.” Alice snorted.

“We weren’t fucking. I’ve never slept with Agatha.” Rio stared at her, as truthful as ever.

“Really?” She frowned.

“Yes.”

“What happened then?” She asked.

“I messed up.”

“How?”

“It’s a long story.”

Alice huffed. “Well, let’s hear it.”

“I messed up and I keep messing up and now she wants nothing to do with me.” Rio crossed her arms over her chest.

“What happened? She was still all over you three weeks ago.”

“It’s complicated.”

“I’m not that dumb.” Alice rolled her eyes.

“Fine.” Rio replied. “You’re so pushy.”

“I know, it’s our job to be.” She smiled.

Rio scoffed at that. She sighed and crossed her forearms over her forehead to try and hide her face from Alice’s scrutinising gaze. “She came over and I thought she was drunk so I told her to sleep over, but she wasn’t and I might have been a bit tipsy, maybe a bit high, I don’t know.” She said very fast.

“Okay.” Alice replied simply. She had not understood a single word.

“And we made out, but I was on my period, so I told her to stop.” She scrunched her nose at the admission.

“Did she?” Her colleague asked worriedly.

Rio was quick to defend Agatha. “Yeah, of course she did. And then I told her I didn’t want her to touch me on my period because it creates a spiritual bond or whatever-”

Alice interrupted her. “Fucking weird.”

“That’s how she reacted too which I didn’t like, okay? She was weirded the fuck out and that shit’s important to me.” Rio started pouting again.

“Sure.” Alice snorted.

“I offered to get her off but she recoiled in horror.”

“Oh yeah, Jen said she wouldn’t let her touch her either.” She said.

“They slept together?” Rio turned to her fully, eyes wide and mouth open in jealousy.

“It was years ago. Calm down.” Alice huffed. “What happened next?”

Rio sat back in her initial position, staring straight ahead. Her colleague would surely call her out on her behaviour. “I told her to forget about it, that it was a bad idea and that we should leave it at that.”

“Right.” She nodded.

“She didn’t like that.”

“Yeah, bet.” She laughed.

“She freaked out and I freaked out and I told her she was a mess and I may have told her she was an alcoholic.” Rio admitted. She closed her eyes, waiting for an impact or an insult.

“What? Why?” Alice asked, confused.

“How many times have I arrested her for disorderly conduct under the influence?” Rio replied, glaring at her.

“Zero time.” Alice stared at her in disbelief.

“What?”

“You’ve arrested her for disorderly conduct, but she was never actually drunk. At best she had two drinks.”

“What?” It was Rio’s turn to be confused now. Nothing made sense.

“You’ve never bothered reading the toxicology reports?”

“No, why would I care? She was never charged with anything anyway.” She said.

“You thought she was drunk? You told her she was a drunk?” Alice repeated. Her tone was accusatory, as if Rio had committed a capital offense.

“Yeah. Okay, in hindsight, that was fucking stupid, but you were there some of the times, she always implied that she was drunk.” She mumbled.

“You fucking idiot.” Alice huffed.

“I know.” Now that she thought about it, the only time she had truly been able to tell Agatha was drunk was during her escapade with Wanda and the Scissoring Mural Incident.

“No, no, you don’t get it. Agatha had a rough time after her son’s death, okay?” Alice sighed. “She actually started drinking, like heavily. She was a mess at that time. She was a mess in class, she was a mess at home, she was a mess everywhere. She spent months in the psych ward.”

Rio stared at her, hearing her words without processing them. “What?” It did not fit the version of Agatha she knew.

Sure, Agatha had implied having a rough time after her son’s passing but never to that extent. Rio knew she had bad days; Rio knew some nights she could not even stand going home, that she would make sure to be anywhere else but there, surrounded by the memory of him.

“Of course you didn’t fucking know that.” Alice scoffed.

“How was I supposed to know that?” She asked.

“I don’t know! You shouldn’t have called her a fucking mess anyway!”

“How many nights has she spent in the cell? Is that not messy?” Rio spat back.

“You idiot, she did it to spend time with you.” Alice replied, raising her voice.

“She was doing dumb shit way before I moved here.” Rio scoffed.

“Look at her fucking file. She was arrested four times in seven years. Tops.”

“How was I supposed to know that?”

“We all knew it! We all knew she was doing it when you were on duty!” Alice let out a humourless laugh.

“And no one told me!”

“We thought you knew, it was fucking obvious.”

“Well, I’m fucking dense.” Rio snapped back.

“You sure are.” She snorted.

Rio sighed and breathed in. She should have known.

No, scratch that.

She already knew that: Agatha had told her so on the anniversary of her son’s death. She had told her about the women she would seek out to avoid going home and she had told her how ending up in the cell had been another way. And as months passed, she would seek less and less women and more and more time with Rio.

How could she forget that?

“So I basically rubbed her son’s death and how much she struggled in her face, is that it?” Rio said.

“Pretty much, yeah.”

“Fuck.” She snapped.

“You could have attacked her on literally anything else. Like the fact that she’s actively blackmailing seven people and running a pyramid scheme and that she’s probably a fence.”

“She is?” Rio frowned.

“Oh yeah. The day we get a warrant for her garage…” Alice laughed at that.

“Do you think she’s going to murder me in my sleep?” Rio asked. It was a potential risk, Agatha loved getting revenge on people who had wronged her. And that day, she had wronged her more than anyone else.

“You should be so lucky. I hope she tortures you for days.”

“Fuck.” She repeated.

“You’re all brawn and no brain.” Alice said. “Let’s get out of here now, we have work to do.”

She led her out of the car and into the station where Rio spent the remaining hours of her shift looking over Agatha’s records.

The reports told no lie: never drunk, never high, never charged. A few arrests had led to her being condemned, minor incidents like that of the bar when Rio had first met her, just Agatha defending herself against wandering hands.

Rio fished older reports, those right after her son’s death. Drunk and disorderly conduct. Resisting arrest. Verbal and physical assault of an officer.

Before that, nothing much. One instance of Agatha attacking another parent whose child had made a comment to her son about having a “whorish” single mother.

Some cases where she was accused of scamming groups of people, but all charges dropped due to a lack of evidence.

And finally, an older, much much older one from Massachusset. Agatha’s mother had died in suspicious circumstances and her only daughter had been the prime suspect. Her death was then ruled a natural cause due to a lack of evidence.

She closed it all and hit her forehead on the desk. Stupid, stupid, stupid. She should have read it all sooner. Why had she not been curious about her past?

Rio went home at the end of her shift, ready to collapse. As she parked in her driveway, she was greeted with quite the sight: the words EVIL WITCH spraypainted on the white frontage of her house, right on the front door.

Her landlord would not like that.

She let out a laugh. Agatha had surely meant it to be insulting but she just found it hilarious.

Resisting the urge to take a picture in front of it, she walked to her mailbox, only to find it smashed to pieces.

All right, she thought. That was inconvenient.

Still, she felt lucky. Agatha had set fire to her neighbours’ flowers for a lesser offense than that.

She needed to beg the woman to accept her apology. Again. Even if it meant letting her go forever. Rio just could not live with the weight of having hurt her.

She fought every non-confrontational cell in her body to drive to the library the next day. She had a speech planned, it was well-structured and concise and genuine and everything she hoped Agatha wanted to hear. She had thought it over, all night really, awake, unable to sleep under the guilt of her own ignorant cruelty.

Agatha scowled as she saw her walk in and step to the counter.

“I’d like to borrow a book.” Rio said, her voice shaking in anxiety.

The librarian huffed. “What book?”

“I don’t know. Do you have recommendations?” She asked. Off script. Agatha was terrifying. She could hardly get the words out. Especially not with Billy standing ten yards away and eavesdropping.

“No.” Agatha replied curtly.

“I’m trying to get into literature. If an expert could help me, that’d be great.” Rio tried grinning but merely managed to pull a face.

“Billiam, help my stalker pick a book and send her on her way. I’m taking my break.” Agatha told the teenager.

She grabbed her coat and handbag to leave the building.

Alright. Change of plans. Again.

“I’ve changed my mind. I don’t need a book, I need some air.” Rio told him before he could offer his help.

She chased after the older woman. “Agatha, please, please, hear me out. Just one minute. Please. I’ll do anything.”

“What the fuck do you want now? Are you here to insult me some more? Something on your mind you haven’t said?” Agatha said, lighting a cigarette. She kept walking, down the steps of the library and towards the street.

“No, I swear. Please, let me explain.” Rio insisted, trotting about to catch up with her long strides.

She huffed. “There’s nothing to explain.”

“It was a misunderstanding.”

Agatha stopped and glared. “No, it wasn’t. I understood you perfectly. You were very clear.”

She pushed past her to continue her walk.

“Please, Agatha. Please.” Rio said. She would have got on her knees had Agatha asked.

“I’m going to get a restraining order against you, you creep.” The librarian replied.

Rio just stopped, stopped walking, stopped trying to follow her. She stopped everything.

“I’ve missed my chance, haven’t I?” She asked in a trembling voice.

It got Agatha to turn around and cackle loudly. “Bold of you to think you had one in the first place.” She taunted.

Rio frowned. “Don’t say that. I know that’s not true.”

The librarian had a cruel glint in her eyes, like she wanted her to hurt. “It was never going to be anything but sex between you and I.”

It almost made Rio cry, it was a fact she had believed for a long while, something she still believed sometimes. The evidence of Agatha’s interest in her was hard to grasp now, entirely intangible. “Don’t lie.” She said through gritted teeth. Her tensed jaw held her tears at bay.

“You think you’re special? Special needs, maybe.” Agatha laughed. “You and I were nothing. I was just trying to fuck you, I would’ve left in the morning.”

“Stop it.” She replied.

“It’s the truth.” Agatha said, looking her right in the eyes.

“No, it’s not.”

She nodded with a sadistic smile. Her eyes did not leave Rio’s face, scrutinising every single micro expression like she was feeding off her pain, like she was deriving some sort of pleasure from breaking her heart.

“I’m sorry I hurt you, okay?” Rio replied screaming. “I’m sorry I said all these horrible things. I didn’t mean to hurt you, I lashed out.” She brought her hands to her face to wipe her tears away. Here she was screaming and crying in the street, begging Agatha for forgiveness.

“Don’t flatter yourself.” She snorted. “You don’t matter enough to hurt me.”

Agatha smiled one last time and turned to walk away.

“I don’t think you’re a mess, okay? I know you’re not a mess. I know you’re not an alcoholic or whatever else I called you!” Rio called after her.

Agatha stopped, it spurred her on. There was her chance to say her peace.

Her mind went blank, the rehearsed speech she had planned was eluding her. She had to improvise.

“I’m fucking dense, you were so right about that. I’m fucking stupid and probably really bad at my job. All those times I arrested you, I did think you were drunk. I didn’t realise you weren’t. And I didn’t know it wasn’t a habit of yours, okay? I didn’t know. I thought you’d always done that, getting arrested every other week. Because I didn’t actually read your dumb police record. I wanted to know you, the real you, the you you would show me.”

Agatha turned around frowning. Her cigarette consumed itself untouched between her fingers.

“I actually think you’re fucking great, like the most interesting person in this town. You’re so smart and funny and hot, okay? I loved talking to you about everything and anything.”

The librarian stared blankly as she spoke, she looked calm, collected, unshakable, while Rio’s nose was running, her tears were falling, her voice was shaky.

“You were the highlight of my shifts every time you got arrested even when you pissed me off.” She admitted.

That was not part of the plan. She was only supposed to apologise, not to make a grand romantic gesture or confess any sort of feelings. Still, it was too late now, she couldn’t take it back or keep the words in.

“And I miss you so much. I miss texting you. I miss talking to you. I miss finding excuses to run into you and spend time with you. I’m the mess, I’m the mess because I couldn’t understand what you were doing. And I will never apologise enough for what I said.”

Rio breathed in and out. Alright. She felt lighter, now. Freer. Less… emotional.

“I was an asshole, I was the biggest bitch in the city, I think you’ve officially lost your title. I know you probably don’t want anything to do with me anymore but I needed you to know that. You’re amazing and I would’ve been so lucky to have you. It would have been my honour.”

She smiled through her tears and raised her hands in defeat.

Agatha hadn’t looked away, she stood unmoving. From a few yards away, Rio could see the tears in her eyes and the way she was biting her cheeks, she could see the way her chest moved, her breaths coming in short gasps.

When she finally spoke, it was with a voice so calm and unemotional it almost felt like a stab to the heart to Rio. “I don’t ever want to see your face again. Don’t come back here.”

She accepted it, the violent blow. She had not expected anything else from her. “Okay.” She whispered. “Okay. I’m sorry.”

She looked at Agatha one last time, just to immortalize her in her mind and walked away. The librarian had made her decision, and she would respect it.

Rio went home, overcome with so much sorrow she could hardly breathe. This was a farewell, there was no more hope, now. No more trying. No more Agatha.

She could not keep salting the other woman’s wounds, nor her own. All that was left for her to do was to move on, she knew it.

She went to her garden and picked the most beautiful flowers she could find, the one that could convey her message.

“Hey, I’m Rio.” She said with an awkward wave.

“No.” Rio replied.

“Not really.” She replied. “It’s basically the same except I used to get called a dyke sometimes.” She shrugged it off as if it meant nothing. It did mean something, but she would be damned before she agreed to appear vulnerable in front of a crowd of Westview residents.

“I’d say I moved to a small town and everyone is too scared to say anything to my face.” She smiled.

“Every time we get asked that question, I just know it’s turning into a fight.” She scoffed.

“Why?” He pushed, believing he would get to the bottom of an eye-opening lesbian interpretation of the heteronormative pattern.

A few people in the audience snorted.

“Okay…” Billy sighed.

“I’m obviously the alpha.” She added, looking at the crowd for validation.

Agatha’s voice was heard from far away. “Lies!”

“Stop eavesdropping, you’re obsessed with me.” Rio screamed back. She turned to the audience. “That’s the sort of sigma behaviour I’m talking about. She can’t be the man.” She nodded.

The crowd laughed.

“Okay. Who’s got questions?” Billy smiled.

Rio’s glare was intimidating enough that no one dared raise their hand.

“Great.” She smiled and got up. “Bye.”

“You’re not staying for the discussion?” He asked as she walked out.

“I genuinely do not care about your experiences as gays in Westview.” She said.

She heard a few gasps from the crowd as well as a few snorts before walking back to the counter where Agatha was pretending to work.

“Stop telling people you’re the man in the relationship.” She told Rio curtly.

“I am the man in the relationship.” Rio replied.

Then followed an argument relying on too many gender stereotypes and clichés to be written about.

Agatha tried to focus on anything but Billy. Anything but Billy’s outfit specifically. It was hard to miss truly, too colourful to avoid. She busied herself with paperwork, head down, never up.

She worried about potential colour-blindness on his part. It was the only logical explanation for this. Why else would he be wearing bright blue and green tie and dye skinny jeans with a tight cropped multicolour polo?

It was not just the clothes, it was the accessories too: whatever pastel grunge look he was hoping for fell through. “Whatever.” Agatha said.

Three weeks later, the librarian breached the subject again.

“Get rid of the house.” She said.

“Why?” Rio asked, munching on an expired granola bar she had found in the cupboard. By that point, she had not slept in her own house in weeks.

“Because it’s ridiculous of you to keep paying rent when you’re here all the time.” Agatha replied.

“No.” She refused.

“I will evict you.”

“You can’t evict me.”

“The rent you pay is too cheap; I’m actually losing money on that property.” Agatha argued.

“Sucks to be you.” Rio snorted.

The librarian learned from her mistakes, she agreed to give Rio what she wanted: a formal proposal.

“Please, move in with me.” She asked during a candlelit dinner.

“Why?” Rio asked, suspicious.

“Because I want you here.” Agatha said.

Rio smiled. And then, one last test: “Can I see your garage?”

“Absolutely not.” The librarian refused.

“Then, no.”

“Oh, come on! I’m doing this to protect you!” Agatha huffed.

“You can’t expect me to move in and not give me access to the garage.” Rio rolled her eyes.

“It’s plausible deniability on your part!”

“You’ve asked me to leave your house for a few hours to get a delivery. You can’t just do that if I’m moving here permanently.” She told her. Being pushed out the door at nine in the morning on a Sunday was not her idea of domesticity.

“Why not?” Agatha scowled.

“Agatha, this is your house. I would feel uncomfortable.” Rio shrugged.

“How can I fix that? What do you want? Do you want to change it all up? Redecorate the inside? What rooms do you want to change?” She said.

“It’s not about that.” Rio frowned.

“What is it about?”

“I’ve never lived with anyone.” She admitted.

“We’ve basically been living together for months.”

“What if you break up with me and I end up homeless?” She said.

Agatha scoffed. “Plenty of people who live together break up and no one ends up homeless.”

The librarian knew that whatever breakup they might have would be the messiest in the entire state. There would be no living under the same roof, even for a short period of time. Rio had a point.

“I’ll have nothing here, though. How am I going to find a house in Westview quickly enough if you throw me out?” Rio argued.

“How about you buy yours from me? You get it for a fair price and put tenants in there and then, if something happens, you throw them out and move back there!” She offered at once.

“I don’t know… I’m not into that property type of shit.” Rio pouted.

“My property manager will take care of it if you pay him ten percent of what you’ll receive in rent.” She said.

“I need to think about it.” The younger woman replied. It seemed to be a good alternative. Plus, she would become a homeowner. That was good, very adult of her.

“Okay.” Agatha smiled.

They finished dinner, had dessert and snuggled on the couch in front of a series Agatha had Torrented. The woman refused to pay for a streaming service. She even used Rio’s Spotify account. She had upgraded her offer to a family plan to accommodate her.

“Are you done thinking about it?” Agatha asked after the opening theme of Amandaland.

“It’s been an hour.” Rio replied.

“So?”

“What if we get in a fight?” The officer sighed.

“When was the last time we had a fight?” Agatha asked as if it were the most preposterous argument she had ever heard.

“Four days ago when I found all these pictures and videos of you fucking other women on your computer.” Rio glared.

Agatha laughed uncomfortably. “They were taken years ago, it’s just blackmail material. You overreacted.” She said. It had been an accident, truly, her Blackmail folder was meant to be hidden in her files, Rio had just happened to click on the wrong window and opened it all.

“You fucked half of Westview including the captain’s wife.” Rio groaned.

Agatha had never hidden her love for seducing married women and using it to her advantage but having photographic evidence and seeing them all in town the next day made her furious. She only had so much self-control, she might just snap and do worse than glare at them at the farmer’s market.

“Most of your Instagram followers are your exes and the rest are the guys who cucked while you fucked their girlfriends.” Agatha fired back.

“Not true, you’re exaggerating, my number is way lower than your number.” She scoffed.

“I didn’t give a shit about any of these women, you actually dated them.” The librarian said.

“See, that’s another fight.” Rio rolled her eyes.

“It’s an argument.” She corrected her.

“Whatever.” Rio shook her head.

“When was the last time we fought apart from that?” Agatha asked.

“Three weeks ago, when you received seven tickets for parking in the handicapped spot in front of the library and tried to get me to get rid of them.”

“What’s the point in dating the police if you can’t enjoy a few perks?” She scoffed.

Rio agreed to move in two days later, because Agatha showed her a concept design sketch of the Witchcraft room she would set up for her in Nicky’s old room and made space for all fifty of her plants around the house.

She freed a shelf for Rio’s spells in the freezer and for the raw materials she used for her homemade products in her cupboards. The garage remained out of bound for her though.

Half of Agatha’s yard was turned into a vegetable garden and the other half bloomed with flowers. She was fine with it, as long as she had enough space left to pull out a sun lounger from which she would be able to stare at Rio while she gardened. Nothing hotter than a woman getting her hands dirty.

Rio bought the house after getting it appraised and never heard from it again. Agatha’s property manager was so efficient it was let after a few weeks. Her living there had allowed the town to forget about the previous dead tenant apparently.

“Do you want a Samsung Z-flip?” Agatha asked when she walked through the door after a shift at the station.

“No? I have an iPhone.” Rio replied. The brand-new phone was still in its box on the kitchen table.

“Fine, I’ll just put it back in the garage later.” The older woman shrugged. She went back to her simmering pot.

“You have phones in there?” Rio asked.

“It doesn’t concern you, my beautiful princess.” Agatha grinned. She used that sickly sweet voice of hers.

“Agatha.” The officer groaned. She was so condescending.

She left her pot to pull up a chair and gestured for Rio to sit. “You shouldn’t worry about such things, just sit here and look pretty, there’s no need for you to form a single thought.” For effect, she stroked her hair and kissed her forehead.

“You’re an asshole.” Rio scoffed.

“Do you want an air fryer?” Agatha asked.

“Air fryers are the worst thing to happen to the cooking industry since that terrible Cookeo machine.”

“Fucking Hamish.” She mumbled, shaking her head.

“When will you let me see the garage?” Rio asked for the hundredth time that month.

“The day you find a way not to be asked to testify against me if the police get enough evidence to arrest me.” Agatha smiled.

Now she, personally, could think of one but with the way Rio reacted to Agatha asking her to move in with her “for convenience’s sake”, the librarian doubted she would get a positive reaction.

“I’m not a snitch.” The officer said.

Chapter Notes

this is the last real chapter of this story, part 17 will be a bit different, get ready to discover the official Westview Library Facebook page (cid:0) i hope you enjoy it, i wrote whatever i wanted bc i can - it's what i've been doing for the past seven weeks

“One time…” Rio started.

Agatha put glass down. Whatever sentence she was starting would end with Agatha dumbfounded and too confused to hold an object still.

She had grown to fear for whatever was to come every time Rio pronounced the words “one time”. It would always end up being a terribly traumatising anecdote or an explicit confession. Agatha would be left gaping and worrying about Rio’s mental state. She had to be clinically insane to volunteer these particular pieces of information unprompted.

One time, I went on one of these water slides that shoot you straight down and the guy told me to cross my legs, except I hate it when men try to tell me what to do so I didn’t cross my legs, and water went up my bum and I shat myself at the park. I trusted a fart in line for another slide and I really should not have.

One time, I went to a sex club and there was an orgy going on so I partook. I just fucked the one lady really.

One time, some drug addict was holding my colleague hostage at work so I shot him in the leg. When he didn’t let go, I shot him in the other leg. And that didn’t work so I had to shoot him in the shoulder. He died from the bleeding because he refused to let us call an ambulance.

One time, I had a threesome with two women while their husbands watched.

Agatha braced herself for what was to come this time.

“One time, I was dating this woman, it wasn’t serious or anything, it had just been a few months but we were supposed to be exclusive. I found out she was cheating on me but she didn’t know I knew so I decided to mess with her. She was really insecure about her weight. I honestly thought she was hot as fuck but I pretended to break up with her over it.”

Rio smiled with empty eyes, the kind of look that made Agatha want to reach for her phone and blindly contact the authorities to have that woman committed.

“You know what? That’s fair.” She said instead of telling Rio she was insane.

“It’s a good thing you won’t cheat on me because I’d have your ass in the mental hospital for months.” The younger woman used that very same smile. It seemed she was trying to send a message across.

Agatha knew very well everything that Rio could use against her were she to stray from the path. Frankly, she was conscious of the vulnerability she had shown the other woman – she had made the decision because she trusted her; but there was always that tiny risk. Good thing she would never cheat on Rio.

“Psycho.” She said.

“Please, you would probably do worse if I cheated on you.” Rio laughed.

Agatha pondered the thought for a moment. “I’d get you.” She nodded. “Accessory to fencing and blackmail and everything else. I’d confess and agree to serve a short sentence if it meant ruining your life.”

Rio seemed impressed at that. “Fair enough.” She smiled. “Good thing I don’t plan on cheating on you. Or even leaving you. If you want to get rid of me, you’ll have to kill me.”

“Sure.” Agatha replied. Till death do us part or whatever they say.

“You want a reading?” Rio grabbed the tarot cards on the coffee table.

She could do readings now. Agatha had barged into Lilia’s shop to intimidate her into giving Rio lessons on Tarot. They had been fruitful apparently – it was well-worth the extortion, in Agatha’s opinion. Rio had called it the best Christmas gift ever.

“Fine but just one card.” She said.

The younger woman shuffled the deck and held it for her to pick one card. Agatha knew the score: she needed to ask a question. “Is Rio ever going to cheat on me?” She said, rolling her eyes.

“You don’t need to ask the cards that.” Rio replied.

Agatha picked the card and looked at it. She knew she had to show Rio first but she was just curious. “Ten of Cups.” She said.

“Ten of Cups? Upright or reversed.” Rio asked.

“I don’t know.” Agatha shrugged.

“Didn’t you pay attention?” She insisted, quite alarmed.

“No?” The librarian sighed.

“Think about it.”

“Why does it matter?” She asked.

“One of them is a very good thing and the other means the opposite.” Rio told her. She looked agitated, Agatha could already feel a migraine coming – she was not dealing with an emotional outburst caused by a Tarot reading. Not on a Tuesday night.

“I don’t know!” She snapped.

“Are you going to break up with me?” Rio asked tearfully.

“Of course not!” She said.

“Do you promise?” The younger woman held her pinkie up.

“Yes, I promise.” Agatha replied, linking her finger with Rio’s.

“Are you going to stay with me forever?” She asked.

“Sure.” The librarian shrugged.

“I’d like a little bit more enthusiasm.” She said.

“Yes, possibly, I don’t see myself with anyone but you.” Agatha admitted, rolling her eyes. It was embarrassing, all the cheesy things Rio made her say.

“Aw.” The younger woman beamed.

“Grow up.” She rolled her eyes.

“I want to fuck you.” Rio told her. Confessions of love always aroused her.

“Great.” Agatha grabbed the deck and threw it across the room before pushing Rio against the cushions of the couch. “I think it was upright.” She said, her head in her neck.

“Check-fucking-mate, my love.” Rio laughed.

Agatha chose to ignore these particular words and whatever Rio meant by them, much too distracted by her wandering hands and her ever so soft skin.

“I’m never drinking again.” Rio groaned into the pillow.

“Quitter mentality.” Agatha replied.

The younger woman turned to her: “You went to rehab, Agatha.” She said, rolling her eyes.

“And yet, here I am, hungover.” Agatha snorted.

She could only blame herself, truly. When Rio asked her what she wanted to do for her birthday, she replied ‘getting drunk’ and Rio had obliged. The evening had started with drinks at home, followed by drinks at the bar and the cheesiest waffle fries there were. Then, it seemed, they had walked home, all three miles, and drunk some more. Agatha was certain edibles had made an apparition at some point during the night.

There they lay now: hungover at ten in the morning on a Thursday morning.

On each of the nightstands were tall glasses of water and on the floor, two buckets. They were both empty, that was one positive point. Agatha was certain that these were Rio’s idea – she was not the kind to plan her morning-afters. She was used to waking up and hating herself.

“The fuck?” Rio whispered, she was met with a post it note as she reached for her glass of water.

“If you’re going to be sick, please use the bucket.” Agatha said before turning to her own glass. “Huh?” She grabbed a note similar to Rio’s.

“Do you remember any of this?” The younger woman asked.

“I’m sure it was your idea.” Agatha growled.

“It’s your handwriting.” Rio replied.

“And that one is yours.” She held the paper from her own nightstand. The note read GET MARRIED! in thick sharpie, Rio’s was identical.

“I’m too hungover to discuss this right now.” The detective said. She left the bed without ceremony, groaning at each movement.

“What? Are you saying no?” Agatha gasped in offense.

“No, I’m saying I need to throw up and sit in the shower for twenty minutes to think about all the decisions that have led us to this.” She replied.

“Fair enough.”

As it turned out, she felt no need to vomit, she just had a very long shower during which she tried her very best to recollect the memories of them. She could hardly remember the way home, just that they had walked and ended up there.

Rio furiously brushed her teeth, impressed to realise that they had removed their makeup the night before and put their clothes away.

“I think we got arrested yesterday.” She said.

“What?” Rio gasped.

Agatha showed her a picture of them in the backseat of a police car and another one with Joseph and Julian.

“This is humiliating.” Rio groaned.

“Oh, there’s a video.”

The footage was not too helpful, there were giggles and embarrassing singing. It seemed they had not been arrested but instead found walking in the street with their stilettos in their hands and driven home to ensure their safety.

“Why didn’t we take an uber?” Rio asked.

“I think you wanted to walk around for a bit, you said it was relaxing.”

“Sounds like something I’d say.” She nodded. “I found more notes in the bathroom.” She added.

“Did we stick them all around the house?” Agatha frowned.

“Possibly.”

“Well…” She smiled. “Can’t say no to you.”

“Good.” Agatha nodded.

“Fine.” She shrugged.

“I am and I will make you beg for my cock.” The detective told her.

“How dost thou, Benedick, the married man?” He quoted at Agatha.

“Oh, hello, Billiam.” She replied. Of course he had been told already.

“Did you actually get married?” He asked excitedly.

“Indeed, we did.” Rio said, displaying her wedding ring.

“Congratulations! I’m so happy for you.” He hugged them both at the same time, squeezing their bodies against him and bouncing around.

“Yeah, yeah.” She mocked.

“Oh the narcissism, look at you making our special day all about you.” Agatha snorted.

“It’s not narcissism to want to share a special day with people you love.” He told them, holding one of their hands in his.

“Ew, Billy.” Rio said, scrunching her nose again.

“So embarrassing.” Agatha replied.

“What happened?” Shai sent.

“Sounds about right.” Her wife nodded.

“Rio, are you okay?” Alice immediately asked as she walked into the station.

“Yeah, never better.” She smiled.

“What about your post on Instagram?” The younger woman asked worriedly.

“Oh, yeah, Agatha’s not my girlfriend anymore, she’s my wife.” She told her, showing her ringed finger.

“You got married?” Alice gasped.

“Yeah. We eloped on Thursday.” Rio grinned.

“Oh my god! Congratulations!” Alice hugged her and Rio let her because she was that happy. “I didn’t know you were engaged.”

“We weren’t, it was a spur of the moment kind of thing.” She shrugged.

“I can’t believe it! You got Agatha to marry you! That’s like beating the final boss in a game where no one can reach the first level.” Alice laughed.

“Well, yes. I’m just special.” Rio replied. She was glad her wife was not there to add ‘special needs’ to her sentence. Agatha was annoying like that.
